This is then the longest time scale which can be explored given the budget, and that for which the best possible sampling efficiency should be obtained. It corresponds to a frequency of 0.5 cm
-1
.
Note how both of these estimates need not be exceedingly precise: changing either by a factor of two would affect minimally the accuracy over the accessible frequency range.

{Answer}

[ref=ex:gle-fermi]
Given that the eigenvalues of the positive-definite matrix
\mathbf{M}
are equal to the square of the dynamical frequencies, and that
\left<p^{2}\right>=T
(assuming unit mass and
k_{B}=1
) one must have
T^{\star}(\omega)=f_{\epsilon,\beta}(\omega^{2})
.

{Answer}

[ref=ex:pigle-limit]
First, consider the case of
\omega\rightarrow 0
.
The term in the sum is zero for all
k
except
k=0
(for which
\omega_{0}^{2}/\omega^{2}=1
). Given that the right-hand side of the equation tends to one,

T^{\star}(\omega)=PT,

(193)

i.e. for low frequencies the target temperature should be that of a conventional PIMD simulation.
In the high-
\omega
limit, and for
P=2
, Eq. (
186
) reads

\frac{1}{2}\left[T^{\star}(\omega)+\frac{\omega^{2}T^{\star}\left(\sqrt{\omega^{2}+4\omega_{P}^{2}}\right)}{\omega^{2}+4\omega_{P}^{2}}\right]\approx\frac{\hbar\omega}{2k_{B}}.

(194)

Expanding the second term around
\omega
one finds that the left-hand side is
T^{\star}(\omega)+\mathcal{O}(1/\omega)
, showing that in the high-frequency limit
T^{\star}(\omega)\approx\hbar\omega/2k_{B}
.

\setchapterpreamble
[u]
\margintoc

Chapter 6
Adiabatic Ring Polymer Rate Theory

\labch

rates-adiabatic

This Chapter and the next provide an introduction to the quantum mechanical theory of chemical reaction rates, and how these rates can be approximated using path integral methods. Although the theory will be presented using one-dimensional notation, the methods we shall describe are easily generalised to treat arbitrarily complex reactions in their full dimensionality. In particular, they do not make any assumptions about the dominance of a single reaction pathway: all contributing pathways are sampled with the appropriate Boltzmann weights. This sets the present methods apart from the ring polymer instanton methods, which are only applicable to relatively simple reactions that are dominated by a single tunneling pathway.

Here we shall consider electronically adiabatic reactions that proceed on a single Born-Oppenheimer potential energy surface, and devote Chapter
to the case of non-adiabatic reactions. Since both cases have been discussed in a recent review article
[
122
]
, we shall keep the presentation here to a minimum, with a focus on using exercises to illustrate the material. More details can be found in Ref.
[
122
]
, and in the other references we shall give as we go along. (See also these Oxford lecture notes
[
123
]
, which show how the flux-side correlation function formulation of reaction rate theory in Eq.
196
can be derived from quantum scattering theory.)

23
Quantum Rate Theory

The simplest possible model for a (bimolecular) chemical reaction is one-dimensional barrier transmission problem with a Hamiltonian of the form

\hat{H}={\hat{p}^{2}\over 2m}+V(\hat{q}),

(195)

in which the potential
V(q)
tends to zero as
q\to-\infty
(the reactant asymptote) and to a constant as
q\to\infty
(the product asymptote). The exact quantum mechanical thermal rate constant for this reaction can be written as
[
124
]

k(T)={1\over Q_{r}(T)}\lim_{t\to\infty}\bar{c}_{fs}(t),

(196)

where
Q_{r}(T)=\sqrt{mk_{\text{B}}T/2\pi\hbar^{2}}
is the reactant partition function per unit length (it would become the reactant partition function per unit volume in three-dimensional space) and
\bar{c}_{fs}(t)
is a flux-side correlation function

\bar{c}_{fs}(t)={\text{tr}}\left[e^{-\beta\hat{H}/2}\hat{F}e^{-\beta\hat{H}/2}e^{+i\hat{H}t/\hbar}\hat{\theta}\,e^{-i\hat{H}t/\hbar}\right],

(197)

with
\beta=1/k_{\text{B}}T
. Here the flux operator

\hat{F}={i\over\hbar}\left[\hat{H},\hat{\theta}\right]

(198)

is the Heisenberg time derivative of a projection operator onto the product side of a dividing surface between the reactants and products at
q=q^{\ddagger}
,

\hat{\theta}=\theta(\hat{q}-q^{\ddagger}),

(199)

and
\theta(x)
is a Heavyside step function.

Figure 32:
A model one-dimensional barrier transmission problem.

Exercise 23.1

(label=ex:rate-exercise1,title=Quantum rate theory)
.

\Question

Show that
k(T)
in Eq.
196
has the correct dimensions for a bimolecular rate constant in one-dimensional space, in which concentrations are measured in terms of the number of particles per unit length.

\Question
Show that the flux operator in Eq.
198
can be written equivalently as

\hat{F}={1\over 2m}\left[\hat{p}\,\delta(\hat{q}-q^{\ddagger})+\delta(\hat{q}-q^{\ddagger})\,\hat{p}\right].

(200)

\Question

Use the definition of
\hat{F}
in Eq.
198
to prove that

\lim_{t\to 0}\bar{c}_{fs}(t)=0.

(201)

\Question

Show that
k(T)
in Eq.
196
can be written equivalently as

k(T)={1\over Q_{r}(T)}\int_{0}^{\infty}\bar{c}_{ff}(t)\,{\mathrm{d}}t,

(202)

where
\bar{c}_{ff}(t)
is the flux-flux correlation function

\bar{c}_{ff}(t)={\text{tr}}\left[e^{-\beta\hat{H}/2}\hat{F}e^{-\beta\hat{H}/2}e^{+i\hat{H}t/\hbar}\hat{F}\,e^{-i\hat{H}t/\hbar}\right].

(203)

\Question

Show that
\bar{c}_{ff}(t)
is an even function of
t
, and hence that the rate constant can also be written as

k(T)={1\over 2Q_{r}(T)}\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\bar{c}_{ff}(t)\,{\mathrm{d}}t.

(204)

\Question

Show that this the same as

k(T)={1\over 2Q_{r}(T)}\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\tilde{c}_{ff}(t)\,{\mathrm{d}}t,

(205)

where
\tilde{c}_{ff}(t)
is the Kubo-transformed correlation function

\tilde{c}_{ff}(t)={1\over\beta}\int_{0}^{\beta}{\mathrm{d}}\lambda\,{\text{tr}}\left[e^{-(\beta-\lambda)\hat{H}}\hat{F}e^{-\lambda\hat{H}}e^{+i\hat{H}t/\hbar}\hat{F}\,e^{-i\hat{H}t/\hbar}\right].

(206)

\Question

(Harder!)
Obtain an explicit expression for
\bar{c}_{fs}(t)
in the case of free-particle motion [where
V(q)=0
], and plot it as a function of
t/\beta\hbar
in the range
[-3,+3]
.

24
Classical Rate Theory

In the classical limit, the trace in Eq.
197
becomes a phase-space average, the operators within the trace become functions of the phase space variables, and the quantum evolution operators are replaced by classical time evolution. The classical limit of the rate constant is thus
[
123
]

k^{\text{cl}}(T)={1\over Q_{r}(T)}\lim_{t\to\infty}c^{\text{cl}}_{fs}(t),

(207)

where
c^{\text{cl}}_{fs}(t)
is the classical flux-side correlation function

c^{\text{cl}}_{fs}(t)={1\over 2\pi\hbar}\int{\mathrm{d}}p_{0}\int{\mathrm{d}}q_{0}\,e^{-\beta H(p_{0},q_{0})}\delta(q_{0}-q^{\ddagger}){p_{0}\over m}\theta(q_{t}-q^{\ddagger}).

(208)

Here
H(p,q)=p^{2}/2m+V(q)
is the classical Hamiltonian and
q_{t}\equiv q_{t}(p_{0},q_{0})
is the position at time
t
of a classical trajectory generated by this Hamiltonian that starts out at the initial phase space point
(p_{0},q_{0})
at time 0. As in the quantum case, the rate constant is obtained from a Boltzmann average of the correlation between the flux through the dividing surface at time 0 [
\delta(q_{0}-q^{\ddagger})p_{0}/m
] and the population on the product side of the dividing surface in the long-time limit [
\lim_{t\to\infty}\theta(q_{t}-q^{\ddagger})
], divided by the reactant partition function per unit length (which is the same in both cases).

Exercise 24.1

(label=ex:rate-exercise2,title=Classical rate theory)
.

\Question

Show that

\lim_{t\to 0_{+}}c^{\text{cl}}_{fs}(t)={1\over 2\pi\beta\hbar}e^{-\beta V(q^{\ddagger})},

(209)

and hence that classical rate theory has the well-defined transition state theory limit

k_{\text{cl}}^{\text{TST}}(T)={1\over Q_{r}(T)}\lim_{t\to 0_{+}}c_{fs}^{\text{cl}}(t)\equiv{k_{\text{B}}T\over h}{1\over Q_{r}(T)}e^{-V(q^{\ddagger})/k_{\text{B}}T}.

(210)

What is the analogous expression in quantum rate theory?

\Question
Show that the classical transition state theory rate constant can be interpreted as

k_{\text{cl}}^{\text{TST}}(T)={1\over 2}\left<|\dot{q}|\right>_{\text{cl}}e^{-\beta V(q^{\ddagger})},

(211)

where

{1\over 2}\left<|\dot{q}|\right>_{\text{cl}}={\displaystyle{{1\over 2}\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}{\mathrm{d}}p\,{|p|\over m}e^{-\beta p^{2}/2m}}\over\displaystyle{\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}{\mathrm{d}}p\,e^{-\beta p^{2}/2m}}}

(212)

is the thermal expectation value of the forward classical flux through the dividing surface and
e^{-\beta V(q^{\ddagger})}
is the probability that a thermal fluctuation will bring the classical system to the top of the reaction barrier.

25
RPMD Rate Theory

As discussed in the lecture on RPMD, ring polymer molecular dynamics is simply classical molecular dynamics in the extended phase space of the ring polymer expression for the quantum mechanical partition function. So ring polymer rate theory is simply classical rate theory in this extended phase space.

For the present one-dimensional barrier transmission problem, the RPMD rate constant can be written in a variety of different ways, depending on the choice of the dividing surface between reactants and products in ring polymer coordinate space
[
125
,
126
]
. The most general expression is

k^{\text{RPMD}}(T)={1\over Q_{r}(T)}\lim_{t\to\infty}c^{\text{RPMD}}_{fs}(t),

(213)

where the RPMD flux-side correlation function is defined as

c_{fs}^{\text{RPMD}}(t)={1\over(2\pi\hbar)^{P}}\int{\mathrm{d}}{\mathbf{p}}_{0}\int{\mathrm{d}}{\mathbf{q}}_{0}\,e^{-\beta_{P}H_{P}({\mathbf{p}}_{0},{\mathbf{q}}_{0})}\dot{\theta}[s({\mathbf{q}}_{0})]\theta[s({\mathbf{q}}_{t})],

(214)

with

\dot{\theta}[s({\mathbf{q}})]=\delta[s({\mathbf{q}})]\sum_{j=1}^{P}{\partial s({\mathbf{q}})\over\partial q_{j}}\cdot{p_{j}\over m}.

(215)

Here
s({\mathbf{q}})=0
is a dividing surface between reactants and products in the ring polymer coordinate space, and
{\mathbf{q}}_{t}\equiv{\mathbf{q}}_{t}({\mathbf{p}}_{0},{\mathbf{q}}_{0})
is the coordinate at time
t
of a trajectory generated by the classical ring polymer Hamiltonian

H_{P}({\mathbf{p}},{\mathbf{q}})=\sum_{j=1}^{P}\left[{p_{j}^{2}\over 2m}+{1\over 2}m\omega_{P}^{2}(q_{j}-q_{j-1})^{2}+V(q_{j})\right]

(216)

from the initial phase space point
({\mathbf{p}}_{0},{\mathbf{q}}_{0})
at time
t=0
. [In Eq.
216
,
\omega_{P}=1/(\beta_{P}\hbar)
with
\beta_{P}=\beta/P
, and
q_{0}\equiv q_{P}
, as is usual in RPMD.]

Figure 33:
Illustration of RPMD rate theory.

Any choice of the dividing surface
s({\mathbf{q}})=0
will give the same RPMD rate constant, provided it separates reactants from products. We are therefore free to choose the dividing surface for convenience, and the most convenient choice is to base it on the centroid

\bar{q}={1\over P}\sum_{j=1}^{P}q_{j}.

(217)

In particular, if we set
s({\mathbf{q}})=\bar{q}-q^{\ddagger}
, we find that Eq.
214
reduces to a form that resembles the classical flux-side correlation function in Eq.
208
,

c_{fs}^{\text{RPMD}}(t)={1\over(2\pi\hbar)^{P}}\int{\mathrm{d}}{\mathbf{p}}_{0}\int{\mathrm{d}}{\mathbf{q}}_{0}\,e^{-\beta_{P}H_{P}({\mathbf{p}}_{0},{\mathbf{q}}_{0})}\delta(\bar{q}_{0}-q^{\ddagger}){\bar{p}_{0}\over m}\theta(\bar{q}_{t}-q^{\ddagger}),

(218)

where
\bar{p}_{0}
is the centroid momentum
\bar{p}=(1/P)\sum_{j=1}^{P}p_{j}
evaluated at time zero. With this choice, the (thermal) flux of ring polymer centroids through the dividing surface at
t=0
is correlated with the population of ring polymer centroids on the product side of the dividing surface at time
t
, as illustrated schematically in Fig.
33
.

The more general expression for the flux-side correlation function in Eq.
214
can be used to establish a connection between (the optimum transition state theory approximation to) the RPMD rate and the semiclassical instanton approximation to the rate
[
127
]
, and so used to explain why RPMD works in the deep quantum tunnelling regime
[
69
]
. However, the simplified expression in Eq.
218
is the one that been used in most practical applications of RPMD rate theory, so it is this that we shall focus on in the following exercise.

Exercise 25.1

(label=ex:rate-exercise3,title=RPMD rate theory)
.

Preamble:
The simplest way to do several parts of this exercise is to note that the free ring polymer Hamiltonian

h_{P}({\mathbf{p}},{\mathbf{q}})=\sum_{j=1}^{P}\left[{p_{j}^{2}\over 2m}+{1\over 2}m\omega_{P}^{2}(q_{j}-q_{j-1})^{2}\right]

(219)

can be simplified by transforming the ring polymer momenta and coordinates into the normal mode representation

\tilde{p}_{k}=\sum_{j=1}^{P}p_{j}C_{jk}\quad\hbox{and}\quad\tilde{q}_{k}=\sum_{j=1}^{P}q_{j}C_{jk},

(220)

where in the case of even
P
the elements of the orthogonal transformation matrix are

C_{jk}=\begin{cases}\sqrt{1/P},&k=0\\
\sqrt{2/P}\cos(2\pi jk/P),&1\leq k\leq P/2-1\\
\sqrt{1/P}(-1)^{j},&k=P/2\\
\sqrt{2/P}\sin(2\pi jk/P),&P/2+1\leq k\leq P-1.\\
\end{cases}

(221)

This gives

h_{P}(\tilde{\mathbf{p}},\tilde{\mathbf{q}})=\sum_{k=0}^{P-1}\left[{\tilde{p}_{k}^{2}\over 2m}+{1\over 2}m\omega_{k}^{2}\tilde{q}_{k}^{2}\right],

(222)

where
\omega_{k}=2\omega_{P}\sin(k\pi/P)
for
k=0,\ldots,P-1
. Since the transformation is orthogonal, we have that

\int{\mathrm{d}}{\mathbf{p}}\equiv\int{\mathrm{d}}\tilde{\mathbf{p}}\quad\hbox{and}\int{\mathrm{d}}{\mathbf{q}}\equiv\int{\mathrm{d}}\tilde{\mathbf{q}},

(223)

and that
{\mathbf{p}}\cdot{\mathbf{p}}=\tilde{\mathbf{p}}\cdot\tilde{\mathbf{p}}
and
{\mathbf{q}}\cdot{\mathbf{q}}=\tilde{\mathbf{q}}\cdot\tilde{\mathbf{q}}
, both of which can help with calculations. For example, the fact that
{\mathbf{q}}\cdot{\mathbf{q}}=\tilde{\mathbf{q}}\cdot\tilde{\mathbf{q}}
implies that the ring polymer Hamiltonian of a system with a harmonic potential
V(q)={1\over 2}m\omega^{2}q^{2}
can be written in the normal mode representation as

H_{P}(\tilde{\mathbf{p}},\tilde{\mathbf{q}})=\sum_{k=0}^{P-1}\left[{\tilde{p}_{k}^{2}\over 2m}+{1\over 2}m(\omega_{k}^{2}+\omega^{2})\tilde{q}_{k}^{2}\right].

(224)

\Question

Starting from the expression for
c_{fs}^{\mathrm{R}PMD}(t)
in Eq.
218
, show that

\lim_{t\to 0_{+}}c^{\mathrm{R}PMD}_{fs}(t)={1\over 2}\left<|\dot{q}|\right>_{\mathrm{c}l}Q(q^{\ddagger}),

(225)

where
{1\over 2}\left<|\dot{q}|\right>_{\mathrm{c}l}
is the forward classical flux through the dividing surface defined in Eq.
212
and

Q(q^{\ddagger})={1\over(2\pi\hbar)^{P}}\int{\mathrm{d}}{\mathbf{p}}\int{\mathrm{d}}{\mathbf{q}}\,e^{-\beta_{P}H_{P}({\mathbf{p}},{\mathbf{q}})}\delta(\bar{q}-q^{\ddagger})

(226)

is a centroid-constrained partition function. It follows from this that ring polymer rate theory has a well-defined transition state theory limit,

k^{\mathrm{Q}TST}(T)={1\over Q_{r}(T)}\lim_{t\to 0_{+}}c_{fs}^{\mathrm{R}PMD}(t)={1\over 2}\left<|\dot{q}|\right>_{\mathrm{c}l}{Q(q^{\ddagger})\over Q_{r}(T)},

(227)

which is known in the literature as the “centroid density quantum transition state theory” approximation to the rate constant
[
128
]
.

\Question

Show that if
q_{r}
is in the reactant region where the potential
V(q)
is zero, then
Q(q_{r})=Q_{r}(T)
(the reactant partition function per unit length).

Hint:
The calculation is straightforward for
P=1
, and for
P>1
you can use the identity
\prod_{k=1}^{P-1}2\sin(k\pi/P)=P
.

\Question

Show that the factor of
\displaystyle{Q(q^{\ddagger})\over Q_{r}(T)}
in Eq.
227
can be calculated as

{Q(q^{\ddagger})\over Q_{r}(T)}=e^{-\beta F(q^{\ddagger})}=\exp\left[-\beta{\int_{q_{r}}^{q^{\ddagger}}}F^{\prime}(q)\,{\mathrm{d}}q\right],

(228)

where

F^{\prime}(q)=\left<{1\over P}\sum_{j=1}^{P}{{\mathrm{d}}V(q_{j})\over{\mathrm{d}}q_{j}}\right>_{\bar{q}=q}

(229)

with

\left<(\cdots)\right>_{\bar{q}=q}={\displaystyle{\int{\mathrm{d}}{\mathbf{p}}\int{\mathrm{d}}{\mathbf{q}}\,(\cdots)e^{-\beta_{P}H_{P}({\mathbf{p}},{\mathbf{q}})}\delta(\bar{q}-q)}\over\displaystyle{\int{\mathrm{d}}{\mathbf{p}}\int{\mathrm{d}}{\mathbf{q}}\,e^{-\beta_{P}H_{P}({\mathbf{p}},{\mathbf{q}})}\delta(\bar{q}-q)}}

(230)

is a standard PIMD average with the centroid of the ring polymer constrained at
q
.

\Question

Show that the RPMD rate constant can be written as

k^{\mathrm{R}PMD}(T)=k^{\mathrm{Q}TST}(T)\lim_{t\to\infty}\kappa(t),

(231)

where

\kappa(t)=\sqrt{2\pi\beta m}\left<{\bar{p}_{0}\over m}\theta(\bar{q}_{t}-q^{\ddagger})\right>_{\bar{q}_{0}=q^{\ddagger}}

(232)

is a time-dependent transmission coefficient that allows for recrossing of the transition state dividing surface.

Once
k^{\mathrm{Q}TST}(T)
has been calculated, one thus simply runs RPMD trajectories initialised in the constrained ensemble with
\bar{q}_{0}=q^{\ddagger}
and counts those that go on to form products with weights of
\sqrt{2\pi\beta m}\,(\bar{p}_{0}/m)
to obtain
k^{\mathrm{R}PMD}(T)
. If
k^{\mathrm{Q}TST}(T)
is calculated by thermodynamic integration as in Eq.
228
, the overall scheme is known as the Bennett-Chandler method
[
129
,
130
]
. This works well even for strongly activated reactions in which reaching the transition state is a rare event [i.e, for which
Q(q^{\ddagger})/Q_{r}(T)\sim e^{-\beta V(q^{\ddagger})}
is exponentially small], and it is the basis of all practical RPMD rate theory calculations.

\Question

(Harder!)
Show that, at temperatures above the instanton crossover temperature
T_{\mathrm{c}}=\hbar\omega_{b}/2\pi k_{\mathrm{B}}
, RPMD rate theory gives the exact quantum mechanical result

k(T)Q_{r}(T)={1\over(2\pi\beta\hbar)}{(\beta\hbar\omega_{b}/2)\over\sin(\beta\hbar\omega_{b}/2)}

(233)

for the thermal rate constant of a parabolic barrier
V(q)=-{1\over 2}m\omega_{b}^{2}q^{2}
,
in the limit as the number of beads
P\to\infty
.

This suggests that RPMD rate theory will also correctly capture the “shallow tunnelling” through the parabolic tip of the reaction barrier in more general situations. Below
T_{\mathrm{c}}
, the parabolic approximation to the reaction barrier breaks down, and the tunnelling enters the “instanton” regime. Here the analysis becomes more complicated, but RPMD is also expected to provide a good approximation to the quantum mechanical rate constant by virtue of its connection to semiclassical instanton theory. In particular, Richardson and Althorpe have shown that there is a direct connection between the optimum transition state theory approximation to the RPMD rate constant (obtained by optimising the dividing surface
s({\mathbf{q}})=0
in Eq.
214
) and the rate constant given by the “Im F” version of semiclassical instanton theory
[
69
]
.

26
Additional Comments and Further Reading

Everything we have said above is straightforward to generalise to gas phase bimolecular reactions in their full dimensionality, without making any reduced dimensionality approximations or assuming (as is commonly done in more approximate theories) that the rotations and vibrations of the transition state can be treated separately. The resulting theory automatically includes zero-point energy and tunnelling effects, both of which are known to be important in reactions involving hydrogen atoms
[
122
]
. The implementation of RPMD rate theory for gas phase atom-diatom reactions is described in
[
131
]
, and the generalisation to polyatomic reactions in
[
132
]
. This generalisation has since been implemented in a freely available computer program
[
133
]
that has already been used to calculate the rates of chemical reactions containing as many as nine atoms. We expect that there will be many more applications as machine-learned
ab initio
potential energy surfaces for polyatomic reactions become more widely available.

RPMD rate theory can also be applied to condensed phase reactions, and indeed the first such application (to a system-bath model for condensed phase proton transfer) was reported in the first RPMD rate theory paper
[
125
]
. For a unimolecular reaction,
Q_{r}(T)
becomes the full reactant partition function

Q_{r}(T)={\text{tr}}\left[e^{-\beta\hat{H}}\theta[-s(\hat{\mathbf{q}})]\right]

(234)

rather than the reactant partition function per unit volume, and the RPMD rate is typically calculated after a “plateau time”
t_{\text{p}}
rather than in the limit as
t\to\infty
:

k^{\text{RPMD}}(T)={c_{fs}^{\text{RPMD}}(t_{\text{p}})\over Q_{r}(T)}.

(235)

Here the plateau time is supposed to be longer than the time taken for a typical reactive trajectory initiated at the transition state to leave the barrier region, but short compared with the timescale for the onset of the reverse reaction
[
130
]
. When this separation of time scales is difficult to achieve, one can use the more general expression
[
134
]

k^{\text{RPMD}}(T)={Q_{r}(T)^{-1}c_{fs}^{\text{RPMD}}(t_{\text{p}})\over 1-\left[Q_{r}(T)^{-1}+Q_{p}(T)^{-1}\right]\int_{0}^{t_{\text{p}}}c_{fs}^{\text{RPMD}}(t)\,{\mathrm{d}}t},

(236)

in which
Q_{p}(T)
is the product partition function

Q_{p}(T)={\text{tr}}\left[e^{-\beta\hat{H}}\theta[s(\hat{\mathbf{q}})]\right]

(237)

and the denominator in Eq.
236
accounts for the onset of the reverse reaction. Here
t_{\text{p}}
simply has to be sufficiently large for the right-hand side of Eq.
236
to have reached a plateau value.

This condensed phase version of RPMD rate theory has also seen numerous applications, ranging from early studies of proton transfer in a polar solvent
[
135
]
, and the hopping of muonium atoms between the cavities in hexagonal ice
[
74
]
, to a fully atomistic simulation of enzyme-catalysed hydride transfer in aqueous dihydrofolate reductase
[
136
]
. We expect that it too will see many more applications as accurate machine-learned potentials for reactive condensed phase systems become more widely available.

Exercise answers

{Answer}

[ref=ex:rate-exercise1]

1.

The second order rate equation for a bimolecular reaction is

-{{\mathrm{d}}c_{\text{A}}\over{\mathrm{d}}t}=kc_{\text{A}}c_{\text{B}}.

In one dimension where concentrations are in particles per unit length, this gives
[k]=
LT
-1
. From Eq.
198
,
[\hat{F}]={\text{T}}^{-1}
, and since
\hat{\theta}
is dimensionless,
[c_{fs}(t)]={\text{T}}^{-1}
. The dimensions of
Q_{r}(T)
(the reactant partition function per unit length) are clearly L
-1
, and so Eq.
196
gives
[k]=
LT
-1
as required.

2.

Since
\hat{\theta}=\theta(\hat{q}-q^{\ddagger})
commutes with
V(\hat{q})
,

\hat{F}={i\over\hbar}\left[\hat{H},\hat{\theta}\right]={i\over 2m\hbar}\left[\hat{p}^{2},\hat{\theta}\right]={i\over 2m\hbar}\left(\hat{p}\left[\hat{p},\hat{\theta}\right]+\left[\hat{p},\hat{\theta}\right]\hat{p}\right).

In the position representation,
\hat{p}=-i\hbar\displaystyle{\partial\over\partial q}
,
\hat{\theta}=\theta(q-q^{\ddagger})
, and therefore
\left[\hat{p},\hat{\theta}\right]=-i\hbar\,\delta(q-q^{\ddagger})
, giving

\hat{F}={1\over 2m}\left[\hat{p}\,\delta(\hat{q}-q^{\ddagger})+\delta(\hat{q}-q^{\ddagger})\,\hat{p}\right].

3.

From Eqs.
197
and
198
,

\displaystyle\bar{c}_{fs}(0)

\displaystyle={\text{tr}}\left(e^{-\beta\hat{H}/2}\hat{F}e^{-\beta\hat{H}/2}\hat{\theta}\right)

\displaystyle={i\over\hbar}{\text{tr}}\left(e^{-\beta\hat{H}/2}\left[\hat{H},\hat{\theta}\right]e^{-\beta\hat{H}/2}\hat{\theta}\right)

\displaystyle={i\over\hbar}{\text{tr}}\left(e^{-\beta\hat{H}/2}\hat{H}\hat{\theta}\,e^{-\beta\hat{H}/2}\hat{\theta}-e^{-\beta\hat{H}/2}\hat{\theta}\hat{H}e^{-\beta\hat{H}/2}\hat{\theta}\right)

\displaystyle={i\over\hbar}{\text{tr}}\left(\hat{H}e^{-\beta\hat{H}/2}\hat{\theta}\,e^{-\beta\hat{H}/2}\hat{\theta}-\hat{H}e^{-\beta\hat{H}/2}\hat{\theta}\,e^{-\beta\hat{H}/2}\hat{\theta}\right)

\displaystyle=0,

where we have used the fact that
e^{-\beta\hat{H}/2}
commutes with
\hat{H}
in the first term and the fact that the trace is invariant to a cyclic permutation of the operators within it in the second.

4.

The flux-side and flux-flux correlation functions in Eqs.
197
and
203
are clearly related by

\displaystyle{{\mathrm{d}}\over{\mathrm{d}}t}\bar{c}_{fs}(t)

\displaystyle={{\mathrm{d}}\over{\mathrm{d}}t}{\text{tr}}\left[e^{-\beta\hat{H}/2}\hat{F}e^{-\beta\hat{H}/2}e^{+i\hat{H}t/\hbar}\hat{\theta}\,e^{-i\hat{H}t/\hbar}\right]

\displaystyle={\text{tr}}\left[e^{-\beta\hat{H}/2}\hat{F}e^{-\beta\hat{H}/2}e^{+i\hat{H}t/\hbar}{i\over\hbar}\left[\hat{H},\hat{\theta}\right]\,e^{-i\hat{H}t/\hbar}\right]

\displaystyle={\text{tr}}\left[e^{-\beta\hat{H}/2}\hat{F}e^{-\beta\hat{H}/2}e^{+i\hat{H}t/\hbar}\hat{F}\,e^{-i\hat{H}t/\hbar}\right]

\displaystyle=\bar{c}_{ff}(t).

So

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{\infty}\bar{c}_{ff}(t)\,{\mathrm{d}}t

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{\infty}{{\mathrm{d}}\bar{c}_{fs}(t)\over{\mathrm{d}}t}\,{\mathrm{d}}t=\lim_{t\to\infty}\bar{c}_{fs}(t)-\bar{c}_{fs}(0)=\lim_{t\to\infty}\bar{c}_{fs}(t),

where we have used the result from part (c) in the final step. Hence

\displaystyle k(T)

\displaystyle={1\over Q_{r}(T)}\lim_{t\to\infty}\bar{c}_{fs}(t)={1\over Q_{r}(T)}\int_{0}^{\infty}\bar{c}_{ff}(t)\,{\mathrm{d}}t.

5.

The proof that
\bar{c}_{ff}(t)
is an even function of
t
uses the commutivity of the evolution and Boltzmann operators and the invariance of the trace to a cyclic permutation. From Eq.
203
,

\displaystyle\bar{c}_{ff}(-t)

\displaystyle={\text{tr}}\left[e^{-\beta\hat{H}/2}\hat{F}\,e^{-\beta\hat{H}/2}e^{-i\hat{H}t/\hbar}\hat{F}\,e^{+i\hat{H}t/\hbar}\right]

\displaystyle={\text{tr}}\left[e^{+i\hat{H}t/\hbar}\hat{F}\,e^{-i\hat{H}t/\hbar}e^{-\beta\hat{H}/2}\hat{F}\,e^{-\beta\hat{H}/2}\right]

\displaystyle={\text{tr}}\left[e^{-\beta\hat{H}/2}\hat{F}\,e^{-\beta\hat{H}/2}e^{+i\hat{H}t/\hbar}\hat{F}\,e^{-i\hat{H}t/\hbar}\right]

\displaystyle=\bar{c}_{ff}(t).

Given this, it is clear that

k(T)={1\over Q_{r}(T)}\int_{0}^{\infty}\bar{c}_{ff}(t)\,{\mathrm{d}}t={1\over 2Q_{r}(T)}\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\bar{c}_{ff}(t)\,{\mathrm{d}}t.

6.

The symmetrically thermalised correlation function
\bar{c}_{ff}(t)
and the Kubo-transformed correlation function
\tilde{c}_{ff}(t)
can both be related to the standard quantum mechanical correlation function

c_{ff}(t)={\text{tr}}\left[e^{-\beta\hat{H}}\hat{F}\,e^{+i\hat{H}t/\hbar}\hat{F}\,e^{-i\hat{H}t/\hbar}\right],

which is an analytic function of its argument throughout the strip
0\leq{\mathrm{Im}}(t)\leq\beta\hbar
in the complex
t
plane. [It is undefined outside this strip because the matrix elements of the Boltzmann operator are undefined at negative temperatures unless the spectrum of the Hamiltonian is bounded from above, which is not the case for the Hamiltonian
\hat{H}=\hat{p}^{2}/2m+V(\hat{q})
.] The relationships are

\displaystyle\bar{c}_{ff}(t)

\displaystyle={\text{tr}}\left[e^{-\beta\hat{H}/2}\hat{F}e^{-\beta\hat{H}/2}e^{+i\hat{H}t/\hbar}\hat{F}\,e^{-i\hat{H}t/\hbar}\right]

\displaystyle={\text{tr}}\left[e^{-\beta\hat{H}}\hat{F}e^{+i\hat{H}(t+i\beta\hbar/2)}\hat{F}e^{-i\hat{H}(t+i\beta\hbar/2)/\hbar}\right]

\displaystyle=c_{ff}(t+i\beta\hbar/2)

and

\displaystyle\tilde{c}_{ff}(t)

\displaystyle={1\over\beta}\int_{0}^{\beta}{\mathrm{d}}\lambda\,{\text{tr}}\left[e^{-(\beta-\lambda)\hat{H}}\hat{F}e^{-\lambda\hat{H}}e^{+i\hat{H}t/\hbar}\hat{F}\,e^{-i\hat{H}t/\hbar}\right]

\displaystyle={1\over\beta}\int_{0}^{\beta}{\mathrm{d}}\lambda\,{\text{tr}}\left[e^{-\beta\hat{H}}\hat{F}e^{+i\hat{H}(t+i\lambda\hbar)}\hat{F}e^{-i\hat{H}(t+i\lambda\hbar)/\hbar}\right]

\displaystyle={1\over\beta}\int_{0}^{\beta}{\mathrm{d}}\lambda\,c_{ff}(t+i\lambda\hbar).

It follows from these relationships and the analyticity of
c_{ff}(t)
that the time integrals from
-\infty
to
\infty
of all three correlation functions are the same. For any
\lambda
between 0 and
\beta
,

\displaystyle\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}c_{ff}(t+i\lambda\hbar)\,{\mathrm{d}}t

\displaystyle=\int_{-\infty+i\lambda\hbar}^{\infty+i\lambda\hbar}c_{ff}(\tau)\,{\mathrm{d}}\tau

\displaystyle=\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}c_{ff}(\tau)\,{\mathrm{d}}\tau

\displaystyle\equiv\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}c_{ff}(t)\,{\mathrm{d}}t,

where we have changed the integration variable to
\tau=t+i\lambda\hbar
in the first line, deformed the integration contour down to the real
\tau
axis in the second, and noted that the boundary terms vanish because
c_{ff}(\tau)\to 0
as
{\mathrm{Re}}(\tau)\to\pm\infty
. (If this were not the case the rate constants in Eqs.
204
and
205
would be undefined.)

Hence

\displaystyle\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\bar{c}_{ff}(t)\,{\mathrm{d}}t=\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\tilde{c}_{ff}(t)\,{\mathrm{d}}t,

and we can equally well write the rate constant in terms of either correlation function.

7.

In the case of free particle motion,
\hat{H}={\hat{p}^{2}/2m}
, and using Eq.
200
for the flux operator gives

\displaystyle\bar{c}_{fs}(t)

\displaystyle={\text{tr}}\left[e^{-\beta\hat{H}/2}\hat{F}e^{-\beta\hat{H}/2}e^{+i\hat{H}t/\hbar}\hat{\theta}\,e^{-i\hat{H}t/\hbar}\right]

\displaystyle={\text{tr}}\left[\hat{F}e^{+i\hat{H}\tau/\hbar}\hat{\theta}\,e^{-i\hat{H}\tau^{*}/\hbar}\right]

\displaystyle={1\over 2m}{\text{tr}}\left[\left[\hat{p}\,\delta(\hat{q}-q^{\ddagger})+\delta(\hat{q}-q^{\ddagger})\hat{p}\right]e^{+i\hat{p}^{2}\tau/2m\hbar}\theta(\hat{q}-q^{\ddagger})e^{-i\hat{p}^{2}\tau^{*}/2m\hbar}\right],

where
\tau=t+i\beta\hbar/2
. Evaluating the trace in the basis of coordinate eigenstates gives

\displaystyle\bar{c}_{fs}(t)

\displaystyle={1\over 2m}\int_{q^{\ddagger}}^{\infty}{\mathrm{d}}q\left[\bigl<q^{\ddagger}\bigr|e^{+i\hat{p}^{2}\tau/2m\hbar}\bigl|q\bigr>\bigl<q\bigr|e^{-i\hat{p}^{2}\tau^{*}/2m\hbar}\hat{p}\bigl|q^{\ddagger}\bigr>+{\text{c}.c.}\right]

\displaystyle={1\over m}{\mathrm{Re}}\int_{q^{\ddagger}}^{\infty}{\mathrm{d}}q\,\bigl<q^{\ddagger}\bigr|e^{+i\hat{p}^{2}\tau/2m\hbar}\bigl|q\bigr>\bigl<q\bigr|e^{-i\hat{p}^{2}\tau^{*}/2m\hbar}\hat{p}\bigl|q^{\ddagger}\bigr>.

The required coordinate matrix elements are

\displaystyle\bigl<q^{\ddagger}\bigr|e^{+i\hat{p}^{2}\tau/2m\hbar}\bigl|q\bigr>

\displaystyle=\sqrt{m\over-i2\pi\hbar\tau}e^{-im(q^{\ddagger}-q)^{2}/2\hbar\tau}

\displaystyle\bigl<q\bigr|e^{-i\hat{p}^{2}\tau^{*}/2m\hbar}\hat{p}\bigl|q^{\ddagger}\bigr>

\displaystyle=\sqrt{m\over+i2\pi\hbar\tau^{*}}{m(q-q^{\ddagger})\over\tau^{*}}e^{+im(q-q^{\ddagger})^{2}/2\hbar\tau^{*}}

and therefore

\displaystyle\bar{c}_{fs}(t)

\displaystyle={m\over 2\pi\hbar|\tau|}{\mathrm{Re}}\int_{q^{\ddagger}}^{\infty}{\mathrm{d}}q\,{(q-q^{\ddagger})\over\tau^{*}}e^{-\beta m(q-q^{\ddagger})^{2}/2|\tau|^{2}}

\displaystyle={m\over 2\pi\hbar|\tau|}{\mathrm{Re}}\left({\tau\over\beta m}\right)

\displaystyle={1\over 2\pi\beta\hbar}{(t/\beta\hbar)\over\sqrt{(t/\beta\hbar)^{2}+1/4}}.

Note that this is a real, odd, and continuous function of
t
, which passes through zero at
t=0
and reaches its long-time plateau value within a few multiples of the thermal time
\beta\hbar
. These features are not unique to the free motion case: they are generic features of the exact quantum mechanical flux-side correlation
\bar{c}_{fs}(t)
when the location of the dividing surface (
q^{\ddagger}
) is chosen appropriately.

Figure 34:
Quantum mechanical
\bar{c}_{fs}(t)
for free motion.

{Answer}

[ref=ex:rate-exercise2]

1.

To obtain the short time limit of
c^{\text{cl}}_{fs}(t)
in Eq.
208
, first note that

\displaystyle\lim_{t\to 0_{+}}\delta(q_{0}-q^{\ddagger})\theta(q_{t}-q^{\ddagger})=\delta(q_{0}-q^{\ddagger})\theta(p_{0}).

So

\displaystyle\lim_{t\to 0_{+}}c^{\text{cl}}_{fs}(t)

\displaystyle={1\over 2\pi\hbar}\int{\mathrm{d}}p_{0}\int{\mathrm{d}}q_{0}\,e^{-\beta H(p_{0},q_{0})}\delta(q_{0}-q^{\ddagger}){p_{0}\over m}\theta(p_{0})

\displaystyle={1\over 2\pi\hbar}\int_{0}^{\infty}{\mathrm{d}}p_{0}\,{p_{0}\over m}e^{-\beta p_{0}^{2}/2m}\,\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}{\mathrm{d}}q_{0}\,e^{-\beta V(q_{0})}\delta(q_{0}-q^{\ddagger})

\displaystyle={1\over 2\pi\hbar}\cdot{1\over\beta}\cdot e^{-\beta V(q^{\ddagger})},

which gives the standard (textbook) transition state theory result for our model one-dimensional barrier transmission problem:

k_{\text{cl}}^{\text{TST}}(T)={1\over Q_{r}(T)}\lim_{t\to 0_{+}}c_{fs}^{\text{cl}}(t)\equiv{k_{\text{B}}T\over h}{1\over Q_{r}(T)}e^{-V(q^{\ddagger})/k_{\text{B}}T}.

There is no direct analogue of this result in the quantum case because
\lim_{t\to 0_{+}}\bar{c}_{fs}(t)=0
, as we have shown in Exercises
(3) and (7).

2.

The two elementary integrals in this question give

{1\over 2}\left<|\dot{q}|\right>_{\text{cl}}={\displaystyle{{1\over 2}\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}{\mathrm{d}}p\,{|p|\over m}e^{-\beta p^{2}/2m}}\over\displaystyle{\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}{\mathrm{d}}p\,e^{-\beta p^{2}/2m}}}={\displaystyle{1/\beta}\over\displaystyle{\sqrt{2\pi m/\beta}}}={1\over\sqrt{2\pi\beta m}},

and combining this with the fact that
Q_{r}(T)=\sqrt{m/2\pi\beta\hbar^{2}}
gives

k_{\text{cl}}^{\text{TST}}(T)={1\over 2\pi\beta\hbar Q_{r}(T)}e^{-\beta V(q^{\ddagger})}\equiv{1\over 2}\left<|\dot{q}|\right>_{\text{cl}}e^{-\beta V(q^{\ddagger})},

the interpretation of which is given in the question.

{Answer}

[ref=ex:rate-exercise3]

1.

Beginning as we did in the answer to Exercise
1, we have

\displaystyle\lim_{t\to 0_{+}}c_{fs}^{\text{RPMD}}(t)

\displaystyle={1\over(2\pi\hbar)^{P}}\int{\mathrm{d}}{\mathbf{p}}_{0}\int{\mathrm{d}}{\mathbf{q}}_{0}\,e^{-\beta_{P}H_{P}({\mathbf{p}}_{0},{\mathbf{q}}_{0})}\delta(\bar{q}_{0}-q^{\ddagger}){\bar{p}_{0}\over m}\theta(\bar{p}_{0})

\displaystyle\equiv{1\over 2}\left<|\dot{\bar{q}}|\right>_{\text{RPMD}}Q(q^{\ddagger}),

where we have defined

{1\over 2}\left<|\dot{\bar{q}}|\right>_{\text{RPMD}}={\displaystyle{{1\over 2}\int{\mathrm{d}}{\mathbf{p}}\,{|\bar{p}|\over m}e^{-\beta_{P}|{\mathbf{p}}|^{2}/2m}}\over\displaystyle{\int{\mathrm{d}}{\mathbf{p}}\,e^{-\beta_{P}|{\mathbf{p}}|^{2}/2m}}}

and

Q(q^{\ddagger})={1\over(2\pi\hbar)^{P}}\int{\mathrm{d}}{\mathbf{p}}\int{\mathrm{d}}{\mathbf{q}}\,e^{-\beta_{P}H_{P}({\mathbf{p}},{\mathbf{q}})}\delta(\bar{q}-q^{\ddagger}).

To show that this is the same as Eq.
225
, we therefore have to show that
{1\over 2}\left<|\dot{\bar{q}}|\right>_{\text{RPMD}}={1\over 2}\left<|\dot{q}|\right>_{\text{cl}}
. This can be done by transforming both the numerator and the denominator of
{1\over 2}\left<|\dot{\bar{q}}|\right>_{\text{RPMD}}
into the ring polymer normal mode representation, and noting that
\beta_{P}=\beta/P
and
\bar{p}=\tilde{p}_{0}/\sqrt{P}
:

\displaystyle{1\over 2}\left<|\dot{\bar{q}}|\right>_{\text{RPMD}}

\displaystyle={\displaystyle{{1\over 2}\int{\mathrm{d}}\tilde{\mathbf{p}}\,{|\bar{p}|\over m}e^{-\beta_{P}|\tilde{\mathbf{p}}|^{2}/2m}}\over\displaystyle{\int{\mathrm{d}}\tilde{\mathbf{p}}\,e^{-\beta_{P}|\tilde{\mathbf{p}}|^{2}/2m}}}

\displaystyle={\displaystyle{{1\over 2}\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}{\mathrm{d}}\tilde{p}_{0}\,{|\bar{p}|\over m}e^{-\beta_{P}\tilde{p}_{0}^{2}/2m}}\over\displaystyle{\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}{\mathrm{d}}\tilde{p}_{0}\,e^{-\beta_{P}\tilde{p}_{0}^{2}/2m}}}

\displaystyle={\displaystyle{{1\over 2}\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}{\mathrm{d}}\bar{p}\,{|\bar{p}|\over m}e^{-\beta\bar{p}^{2}/2m}}\over\displaystyle{\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}{\mathrm{d}}\bar{p}\,e^{-\beta\bar{p}^{2}/2m}}}={1\over 2}\left<|\dot{q}|\right>_{\text{cl}}.

2.

Since
q_{r}
is in the reactant region where
V(q)=0
, we can replace the ring-polymer Hamiltonian
H_{P}({\mathbf{p}},{\mathbf{q}})
with the free ring polymer Hamiltonian
h_{P}({\mathbf{p}},{\mathbf{q}})
in the expression for
Q(q_{r})
[i.e., in Eq.
226
with
q^{\ddagger}\to q_{r}
]. And since
\bar{q}=\tilde{q}_{0}/\sqrt{P}
and
\delta(\tilde{q}_{0}/\sqrt{P}-q_{r})=\sqrt{P}\delta(\tilde{q}_{0}-q_{r}\sqrt{P})
, this gives

\displaystyle Q(q)

\displaystyle={1\over(2\pi\hbar)^{P}}\int{\mathrm{d}}{\mathbf{p}}\int{\mathrm{d}}{\mathbf{q}}\,e^{-\beta_{P}h_{P}({\mathbf{p}},{\mathbf{q}})}\delta(\bar{q}-q)

\displaystyle={1\over(2\pi\hbar)^{P}}\int{\mathrm{d}}\tilde{\mathbf{p}}\int{\mathrm{d}}\tilde{\mathbf{q}}\,e^{-\beta_{P}h_{P}(\tilde{\mathbf{p}},\tilde{\mathbf{q}})}\sqrt{P}\delta(\tilde{q}_{0}-q\sqrt{P})

\displaystyle={1\over(2\pi\hbar)^{P}}\left({2\pi m\over\beta_{P}}\right)^{P/2}\sqrt{P}\left({2\pi\over\beta_{P}m}\right)^{(P-1)/2}\prod_{k=1}^{P-1}{1\over\omega_{k}}

\displaystyle=\sqrt{m\over 2\pi\beta\hbar^{2}}\,P\prod_{k=1}^{P-1}{1\over 2\sin(k\pi/P)}

\displaystyle=Q_{r}(T),

where we have used
\beta_{P}=\beta/P
,
\omega_{k}=(2/\beta_{P}\hbar)\sin(k\pi/P)
, and the identity
\prod_{k=1}^{P-1}2\sin(k\pi/P)=P
to get to the bottom line.

3.

First note that, since
\bar{q}=\tilde{q}_{0}/\sqrt{P}
,

\displaystyle{{\mathrm{d}}\over{\mathrm{d}}\bar{q}}=\sqrt{P}{\partial\over\partial\tilde{q}_{0}}=\sqrt{P}\sum_{j=1}^{P}{\partial q_{j}\over\partial\tilde{q}_{0}}{\partial\over\partial q_{j}}=\sqrt{P}\sum_{j=1}^{P}C_{j0}{\partial\over\partial q_{j}}=\sum_{j=1}^{P}{\partial\over\partial q_{j}},

where we have used the orthogonality of the bead to normal mode transformation to write

\displaystyle q_{j}=\sum_{k=0}^{P-1}C_{jk}\tilde{q}_{k}

and then used the expression for
C_{j0}
in Eq.
221
. Note also that the harmonic spring terms in the ring polymer Hamiltonian

\displaystyle\sum_{j=1}^{P}{1\over 2}m\omega_{P}^{2}(q_{j}-q_{j-1})^{2}=\sum_{k=0}^{P-1}{1\over 2}m\omega_{k}^{2}\tilde{q}_{k}^{2}

are independent of
\tilde{q}_{0}
(and therefore of
\bar{q})
because
\omega_{0}=0
.

In view of these observations, the derivative of the free energy
F(q)=-\displaystyle{1\over\beta}\ln{Q(q)\over Q_{r}(T)}
can be calculated as

\displaystyle F^{\prime}(q)

\displaystyle=-{1\over\beta Q(q)}{{\mathrm{d}}Q(q)\over{\mathrm{d}}q}

\displaystyle=-{1\over 2\pi\hbar\beta Q(q)}\int{\mathrm{d}}{\mathbf{p}}_{0}\int{\mathrm{d}}{\mathbf{q}}_{0}\,e^{-\beta_{P}H_{P}({\mathbf{p}}_{0},{\mathbf{q}}_{0})}{{\mathrm{d}}\over{\mathrm{d}}q}\delta(q-\bar{q})

\displaystyle=-{1\over 2\pi\hbar\beta Q(q)}\int{\mathrm{d}}{\mathbf{p}}_{0}\int{\mathrm{d}}{\mathbf{q}}_{0}\,\delta(q-\bar{q}){{\mathrm{d}}\over{\mathrm{d}}\bar{q}}\,e^{-\beta_{P}H_{P}({\mathbf{p}}_{0},{\mathbf{q}}_{0})}

\displaystyle={1\over 2\pi\hbar\beta Q(q)}\int{\mathrm{d}}{\mathbf{p}}_{0}\int{\mathrm{d}}{\mathbf{q}}_{0}\,\delta(q-\bar{q})\left[\beta_{P}\sum_{j=1}^{P}{{\mathrm{d}}V(q_{j})\over{\mathrm{d}}q_{j}}\right]e^{-\beta_{P}H_{P}({\mathbf{p}}_{0},{\mathbf{q}}_{0})}

\displaystyle={1\over 2\pi\hbar Q(q)}\int{\mathrm{d}}{\mathbf{p}}_{0}\int{\mathrm{d}}{\mathbf{q}}_{0}\,\left[{1\over P}\sum_{j=1}^{P}{{\mathrm{d}}V(q_{j})\over{\mathrm{d}}q_{j}}\right]e^{-\beta_{P}H_{P}({\mathbf{p}}_{0},{\mathbf{q}}_{0})}\delta(q-\bar{q})

\displaystyle\equiv\left<{1\over P}\sum_{j=1}^{P}{{\mathrm{d}}V(q_{j})\over{\mathrm{d}}q_{j}}\right>_{\bar{q}=q},

and in view of the result in part (b), the factor of
Q(q^{\ddagger})/Q_{t}(T)
in
K^{\text{QTST}}(T)
can be calculated as

\displaystyle{Q(q^{\ddagger})\over Q_{t}(T)}=e^{-\beta F(q^{\ddagger})}=\exp\left[-\beta{\int_{q_{r}}^{q^{\ddagger}}}F^{\prime}(q)\,{\mathrm{d}q}\right],

where
q_{r}
is in the reactant region such that
Q(q_{r})=Q_{r}(T)
.

4.

Making use of the results we have already established, we have

\displaystyle\kappa(t)

\displaystyle={c_{fs}^{\text{RPMD}}(t)\over\displaystyle{{1\over 2}\left<|\dot{q}|\right>_{\text{cl}}Q(q^{\ddagger})}}

\displaystyle=\sqrt{2\pi\beta m}\,{c_{fs}^{\text{RPMD}}(t)\over Q(q^{\ddagger})}

\displaystyle=\sqrt{2\pi\beta m}\,{\displaystyle{\int{\mathrm{d}}{\mathbf{p}}_{0}\int{\mathrm{d}}{\mathbf{q}}_{0}\,e^{-\beta_{P}H_{P}({\mathbf{p}}_{0},{\mathbf{q}}_{0})}{\bar{p}_{0}\over m}\delta(\bar{q}_{0}-q^{\ddagger})\theta(\bar{q}_{t}-q^{\ddagger})}\over\displaystyle{\int{\mathrm{d}}{\mathbf{p}}_{0}\int{\mathrm{d}}{\mathbf{q}}_{0}\,e^{-\beta_{P}H_{P}({\mathbf{p}}_{0},{\mathbf{q}}_{0})}\delta(\bar{q}_{0}-q^{\ddagger})}}

\displaystyle\equiv\sqrt{2\pi\beta m}\left<{\bar{p}_{0}\over m}\theta(\bar{q}_{t}-q^{\ddagger})\right>_{\bar{q}_{0}=q^{\ddagger}}.

Choosing
q^{\ddagger}=0
, which is at the top of the parabolic barrier potential
V(q)=-{1\over 2}m\omega_{b}^{2}q^{2}
and therefore the most natural position for the transition state dividing surface, we have

\displaystyle k^{\text{RPMD}}(T)Q_{r}(T)

\displaystyle=\lim_{t\to\infty}c_{fs}^{\text{RPMD}}(t)

\displaystyle=\lim_{t\to\infty}{1\over(2\pi\hbar)^{P}}\int{\mathrm{d}}{\mathbf{p}}\int{\mathrm{d}}{\mathbf{q}}\,e^{-\beta_{P}H_{P}({\mathbf{p}},{\mathbf{q}})}{\bar{p}\over m}\delta(\bar{q})\theta[\bar{q}(t)],

where
\bar{q}(t)
is the centroid of a ring polymer that has evolved from the initial phase space point
({\mathbf{p}},{\mathbf{q}})
for time
t
. Transforming this to the normal mode representation using
\bar{p}=\tilde{p}_{0}/\sqrt{P}
and
\bar{q}=\tilde{q}_{0}/\sqrt{P}
gives

\displaystyle k^{\text{RPMD}}(T)Q_{r}(T)=\lim_{t\to\infty}{1\over(2\pi\hbar)^{P}}\int{\mathrm{d}}\tilde{\mathbf{p}}\int{\mathrm{d}}\tilde{\mathbf{q}}\,e^{-\beta_{P}H_{P}(\tilde{\mathbf{p}},\tilde{\mathbf{q}})}{\tilde{p}_{0}\over m}\delta(\tilde{q}_{0})\theta[\tilde{q}_{0}(t)],

where

\displaystyle H_{P}(\tilde{\mathbf{p}},\tilde{\mathbf{q}})=\sum_{k=0}^{P-1}\left[{\tilde{p}_{k}^{2}\over 2m}+{1\over 2}m(\omega_{k}^{2}-\omega_{b}^{2})\tilde{q}_{k}^{2}\right].

Since the Hamiltonian is diagonal in the normal modes,
k^{\text{RPMD}}(T)Q_{r}(T)
separates into a product of
P
independent phase space integrals

\displaystyle k^{\text{RPMD}}(T)Q_{r}(T)=\prod_{k=0}^{P-1}I_{k},

where

\displaystyle I_{0}

\displaystyle=\lim_{t\to\infty}{1\over 2\pi\hbar}\int{\mathrm{d}}\tilde{p}_{0}\int{\mathrm{d}}\tilde{q}_{0}\,e^{-\beta_{P}[\tilde{p}_{0}^{2}/2m-m\omega_{b}^{2}\tilde{q}_{0}^{2}/2]}{\tilde{p}_{0}\over m}\delta(\tilde{q}_{0})\theta[\tilde{q}_{0}(t)]

\displaystyle=\lim_{t\to\infty}{1\over 2\pi\hbar}\int{\mathrm{d}}\tilde{p}_{0}\,e^{-\beta_{P}\tilde{p}_{0}^{2}/2m}{\tilde{p}_{0}\over m}\theta\left[{\tilde{p}_{0}\sinh(\omega_{b}t)\over m\omega_{b}}\right]

\displaystyle={1\over 2\pi\hbar}\int{\mathrm{d}}\tilde{p}_{0}\,e^{-\beta_{P}\tilde{p}_{0}^{2}/2m}{\tilde{p}_{0}\over m}\theta[\tilde{p}_{0}]

\displaystyle={1\over 2\pi\beta_{P}\hbar},

and

\displaystyle I_{k}

\displaystyle={1\over 2\pi\hbar}\int{\mathrm{d}}\tilde{p}_{k}\int{\mathrm{d}}\tilde{q}_{k}\,e^{-\beta_{P}[\tilde{p}_{k}^{2}/2m+m(\omega_{k}^{2}-\omega_{b}^{2})\tilde{q}_{k}^{2}/2]}

\displaystyle={1\over 2\pi\hbar}\cdot\left({2\pi m\over\beta_{P}}\right)^{1/2}\cdot\left({2\pi\over\beta_{P}m(\omega_{k}^{2}-\omega_{b}^{2})}\right)^{1/2}

\displaystyle={1\over\beta_{P}\hbar(\omega_{k}^{2}-\omega_{b}^{2})^{1/2}},

for
k\geq 1
, provided
\omega_{k}>\omega_{b}
. So if we are above the instanton crossover temperature where
\omega_{1}=(2/\beta_{P}\hbar)\sin(\pi/P)\simeq 2\pi/\beta\hbar>\omega_{b}
(or
T>T_{\text{c}}=\hbar\omega_{b}/2\pi k_{\text{B}}
), we have

\displaystyle k^{\text{RPMD}}(T)Q_{r}(T)

\displaystyle={1\over 2\pi\beta_{P}\hbar}\prod_{k=1}^{P-1}{1\over\beta_{P}\hbar(\omega_{k}^{2}-\omega_{b}^{2})^{1/2}}

\displaystyle={1\over 2\pi\hbar}\,P\prod_{k=1}^{P-1}{1\over[4\sin^{2}(k\pi/P)-(\beta\hbar\omega_{b}/P)^{2}]^{1/2}}.

In order to show that this converges on the exact quantum mechanical result given in the question when we increase the number of ring polymer beads, we still have to prove that

\displaystyle\lim_{P\to\infty}P\prod_{k=1}^{P-1}{1\over[4\sin^{2}(k\pi/P)-(\beta\hbar\omega_{b}/P)^{2}]^{1/2}}={\sin(\beta\hbar\omega_{b}/2)\over(\beta\hbar\omega_{b}/2)}.

The easiest way to do this is to note that the
P
-bead path integral approximation to the partition function of a simple harmonic oscillator with
V(q)={1\over 2}m\omega^{2}q^{2}
converges on the exact quantum mechanical result in the limit as
P\to\infty
, which implies using a similar argument to the one we have just given that
[
125
]

\displaystyle\lim_{P\to\infty}P\prod_{k=0}^{P-1}{1\over[4\sin^{2}(k\pi/P)^{2}+(\beta\hbar\omega/P)^{2}]^{1/2}}={1\over 2\sinh(\beta\hbar\omega/2)}.

Eliminating the first term in the product with
k=0
gives

\displaystyle\lim_{P\to\infty}P\prod_{k=1}^{P-1}{1\over[4\sin^{2}(k\pi/P)^{2}+(\beta\hbar\omega/P)^{2}]^{1/2}}={\beta\hbar\omega/2\over 2\sinh(\beta\hbar\omega/2)},

and setting
\omega=i\omega_{b}
immediately gives the desired result. Hence RPMD does indeed give the exact quantum mechanical rate constant for a parabolic barrier.

\setchapterpreamble

[u]
\margintoc

Chapter 7
Non-adiabatic Ring Polymer Rate Theory

\labch

rates-nonadiabatic

Chapter
has shown how RPMD rate theory can be used to calculate the rates of electronically adiabatic reactions that proceed on a single Born-Oppenheimer potential energy surface.
This Chapter extends the methodology to electronically non-adiabatic reactions in the Fermi Golden Rule limit, and more general non-adiabatic reactions that lie between the Born-Oppenheimer and Fermi Golden Rule extremes.
The extension for non-adiabatic reactions in the Fermi Golden Rule limit was originally suggested by Wolynes in the 1980s, and so predates RPMD rate theory by almost twenty years. However, the treatment of reactions that lie between the Golden Rule and Born-Oppenheimer limits is a much harder problem that is still a topic of ongoing research. In Section
29
we describe a simple interpolation formula that can be used to combine the results of RPMD rate theory and Wolynes theory calculations for reactions in the intermediate regime. This formula is not perfect, but it is simple to implement, and there are good reasons to believe that it is the best one will ever be able to do with ring polymer methods for general electronically non-adiabatic reactions.

27
Fermi’s Golden Rule

Consider an electronically non-adiabatic reaction (such as an electron transfer reaction) that involves a transition between two diabatic electronic states
\left|{0}\right>
and
\left|{1}\right>
. Sticking with one-dimensional notation for simplicity, the generic Hamiltonian for this reaction is

\hat{H}=\hat{H}_{0}\left|{0}\right>\left<{0}\right|+\hat{H}_{1}\left|{1}\right>\left<{1}\right|+{\Delta}\left(\left|{0}\right>\left<{1}\right|+\left|{1}\right>\left<{0}\right|\right),

(238)

where

\hat{H}_{i}={\hat{p}^{2}\over 2m}+V_{i}(\hat{q})

(239)

and we have taken the diabatic electronic coupling matrix element
\Delta
to be a constant, independent of the nuclear coordinate
\hat{q}
(the Condon approximation).

The exact quantum mechanical rate constant for the transition from state
\left|{0}\right>
to state
\left|{1}\right>
can be written in the same way as in Eqs.
196
and
197
,

k(T)={1\over Q_{r}(T)}\lim_{t\to\infty}\bar{c}_{fs}(t),

(240)

where

\bar{c}_{fs}(t)={\text{tr}}\left[e^{-\beta\hat{H}/2}\hat{F}e^{-\beta\hat{H}/2}e^{+i\hat{H}t/\hbar}\hat{\theta}\,e^{-i\hat{H}t/\hbar}\right].

(241)

However the reactant partition function is now

Q_{r}(T)={\text{tr}}\left[e^{-\beta\hat{H}}\left|{0}\right>\left<{0}\right|\right]={\text{tr}}\left<{0}\right|e^{-\beta\hat{H}}\left|{0}\right>,

(242)

(where “Tr” denotes a trace over both the nuclear and the electronic degrees of freedom whereas “tr” denotes a trace over just the nuclear degrees of freedom as in Chapter.
, the projection operator onto products is

\hat{\theta}=\left|{1}\right>\left<{1}\right|,

(243)

and the corresponding reactive flux operator is

\hat{F}={i\over\hbar}\left[\hat{H},\hat{\theta}\right]={i\Delta\over\hbar}\left(\left|{0}\right>\left<{1}\right|-\left|{1}\right>\left<{0}\right|\right).

(244)

The arguments in parts 4,5 and 6 of Exercise
also apply in the present context and show that the rate in Eq.
240
can be written equivalently as

k(T)={1\over 2Q_{r}(T)}\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}c_{ff}(t)\,{\mathrm{d}}t,

(245)

where
c_{ff}(t)
is the standard quantum mechanical flux-flux correlation function

{c}_{ff}(t)={\text{tr}}\left[e^{-\beta\hat{H}}\hat{F}\,e^{+i\hat{H}t/\hbar}\hat{F}\,e^{-i\hat{H}t/\hbar}\right].

(246)

This equivalent expression provides a more convenient way to calculate the rate in the non-adiabatic limit where
\Delta
can be treated as a perturbation. As shown in the following exercise, the leading order contribution to the rate constant in the limit as
\Delta\to 0
is the Golden Rule rate

k(T)={\Delta^{2}\over\hbar^{2}Q_{r}(T)}\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}c_{0}(t)\,{\mathrm{d}}t,

(247)

where

Q_{r}(T)={\text{tr}}\left[e^{-\beta\hat{H}_{0}}\right]

(248)

and

c_{0}(t)={\text{tr}}\left[e^{-\beta\hat{H}_{0}}e^{-i\hat{H}_{0}t/\hbar}e^{+i\hat{H}_{1}t/\hbar}\right].

(249)

Exercise 27.1

(label=ex:rate-exercise4,title=Fermi’s Golden Rule)
.

\Question

Show that the flux-flux correlation function in Eq.
246
can be written as

c_{ff}(t)={\Delta^{2}\over\hbar^{2}}\left[c_{0}(t)+c_{1}(t)+O(\Delta^{2})\right],

(250)

where
c_{0}(t)
is defined in Eq.
249
and

c_{1}(t)={\text{tr}}\left[e^{-\beta\hat{H}_{1}}e^{-i\hat{H}_{1}t/\hbar}e^{+i\hat{H}_{0}t/\hbar}\right].

(251)

This does not immediately lead us to Eq.
247
because
c_{0}(t)
and
c_{1}(t)
are not the same. However, one can show that their time integrals from
-\infty
to
+\infty
are the same for any problem for which the quantum mechanical Golden Rule rate is well defined, thereby completing the derivation of Eq.
247
.

\Question

The quantum mechanical Golden Rule rate is only well defined if one or other (or both) of the Hamiltonians
\hat{H}_{0}
and
\hat{H}_{1}
has a continuous spectrum. This is the case (for example) for a one-dimensional electronic predissociation model with
V_{0}(q)={1\over 2}kq^{2}
and
V_{1}(q)=Ae^{-q/q_{1}}-\varepsilon
. Supposing that
\hat{H}_{0}
has a discrete spectrum and
\hat{H}_{1}
a continuous spectrum as is the case in this predissociation model, show that the time integrals from
-\infty
to
+\infty
of
c_{0}(t)
and
c_{1}(t)
are the same.

\Question

In the classical limit, Eq.
248
becomes

Q_{r}(T)={1\over 2\pi\hbar}\int{\mathrm{d}}p\int{\mathrm{d}}q\,e^{-\beta[p^{2}/2m+V_{0}(q)]},

(252)

Eq.
249
becomes

c_{0}(t)={1\over 2\pi\hbar}\int{\mathrm{d}}p\int{\mathrm{d}}q\,e^{-\beta[p^{2}/2m+V_{0}(q)]}e^{-i[V_{0}(q)-V_{1}(q)]t/\hbar},

(253)

and the Golden Rule rate in Eq.
247
becomes well defined even when the classical motion in both potentials is bounded (because classical densities of states are continuous).

Consider such a problem with
V_{0}(q)={1\over 2}kq^{2}
and
V_{1}(q)={1\over 2}k(q-q_{1})^{2}-\varepsilon
, as illustrated in Fig.
35
. Show that for this problem the classical Golden Rule rate is just the Marcus theory rate

k(T)={\Delta^{2}\over\hbar}\sqrt{\pi\beta\over\Lambda}e^{-\beta(\Lambda-\varepsilon)^{2}/4\Lambda},

(254)

where
\Lambda={1\over 2}kq_{1}^{2}
.

Figure 35:
A one-dimensional spin-boson model.

28
Wolynes Theory

In a pioneering paper published over thirty years ago, Wolynes made a steepest descent approximation to the time integral in Eq.
247
to obtain
[
137
]

k(T)\simeq{\Delta^{2}\over\hbar Q_{r}(T)}\sqrt{2\pi\over-\beta F^{\prime\prime}(\lambda_{\mathrm{sp}})}e^{-\beta F(\lambda_{\mathrm{sp}})},

(255)

where
F(\lambda)=-\ln[c_{0}(i\lambda\hbar)]/\beta
is an effective free energy such that

e^{-\beta F(\lambda)}={\text{tr}}\left[e^{-(\beta-\lambda)\hat{H}_{0}}e^{-\lambda\hat{H}_{1}}\right],

(256)

and
\lambda_{\mathrm{sp}}
can be found from the saddle point condition
F^{\prime}(\lambda_{\mathrm{sp}})=0
. Noting that
e^{-\beta F(0)}={\text{tr}}\left[e^{-\beta\hat{H}_{0}}\right]=Q_{r}(T)
, we see that Eq.
255
can be re-written as

k(T)\simeq{\Delta^{2}\over\hbar}\sqrt{2\pi\over-\beta F^{\prime\prime}(\lambda_{\mathrm{sp}})}\exp\left[-\beta\int_{0}^{\lambda_{\mathrm{sp}}}F^{\prime}(\lambda)\,{\mathrm{d}}\lambda\right].

(257)

Since this only involves free energy derivatives, it is ideally suited to an imaginary time path integral calculation.

A
P
-bead path integral discretisation of Eq.
256
can be constructed in the same way as the standard Trotter product discretisation of an ordinary quantum mechanical partition function
Q={\text{tr}}\left[e^{-\beta\hat{H}}\right]
. The only difference is that we must now pay attention to which beads of the ring polymer necklace are on electronic state
\left|{0}\right>
and which on electronic state
\left|{1}\right>
. This is straightforward to do for
\lambda
in the range
0\leq\lambda\leq\beta
, and when one does it one finds that the result can be written in the form

e^{-\beta F(\lambda_{l})}={1\over(2\pi\hbar)^{P}}\int{\mathrm{d}}{\mathbf{p}}\int{\mathrm{d}}{\mathbf{q}}\,e^{-\beta_{P}H_{P}^{(l)}({\mathbf{p}},{\mathbf{q}})},

(258)

where
\lambda_{l}=l\beta_{P}
for
l=0,\ldots,P
. Here

H_{P}^{(0)}({\mathbf{p}},{\mathbf{q}})=h_{P}({\mathbf{p}},{\mathbf{q}})+\sum_{j=0}^{P}V_{0}(q_{j}),

(259)

H_{P}^{(P)}({\mathbf{p}},{\mathbf{q}})=h_{P}({\mathbf{p}},{\mathbf{q}})+\sum_{j=0}^{P}V_{1}(q_{j}),

(260)

and

H_{P}^{(l)}({\mathbf{p}},{\mathbf{q}})=h_{P}({\mathbf{p}},{\mathbf{q}})+\bar{V}(q_{1})+\sum_{j=2}^{l}V_{1}(q_{j})+\bar{V}(q_{l+1})+\sum_{j=l+2}^{P}V_{0}(q_{j})

(261)

for
l=1,\ldots,P-1
, where
\bar{V}(q)={1\over 2}\left[V_{0}(q)+V_{1}(q)\right]
is the average potential and
h_{P}({\mathbf{p}},{\mathbf{q}})
is the free ring polymer Hamiltonian in Eq.
219
.

Using this discretisation, the derivatives of
F(\lambda)
that are needed to evaluate Eq.
257
can be written as
[
138
]

-\beta F^{\prime}(\lambda_{l})=\left<\delta V(q_{1})\right>_{l}

(262)

and

-\beta F^{\prime\prime}(\lambda_{l})=\left<\delta V(q_{1})\delta V(q_{l+1})\right>_{l}-\left<\delta V(q_{1})\right>_{l}^{2},

(263)

where
\delta V(q)=V_{0}(q)-V_{1}(q)
and

\left<\cdots\right>_{l}={\displaystyle{\int{\mathrm{d}}{\mathbf{p}}\int{\mathrm{d}}{\mathbf{q}}\,e^{-\beta_{P}H_{P}^{(l)}({\mathbf{p}},{\mathbf{q}})}\left(\cdots\right)}\over\displaystyle{\int{\mathrm{d}}{\mathbf{p}}\int{\mathrm{d}}{\mathbf{q}}\,e^{-\beta_{P}H_{P}^{(l)}({\mathbf{p}},{\mathbf{q}})}}}

(264)

is a canonical average in the system with the ring polymer Hamiltonian
H_{P}^{(l)}({\mathbf{p}},{\mathbf{q}})
. For example, when
l=P/2
and
\lambda_{l}=\beta/2
, this system has the ring polymer Hamiltonian illustrated in Fig.
36
.

Figure 36:
Illustration of the path integral used in Wolynes theory when
l=P/2
and
\lambda_{l}=\beta/2
. The hollow ring polymer beads are on the reactant surface (
r
), the filled beads on the product surface (
p
), and the shaded beads on the average of the two.

Exercise 28.1

(label=ex:rate-exercise5,title=Wolynes Theory)
.

\Question

Show that, in the classical limit, Wolynes theory gives the same rate as Marcus theory for the spin-boson model in Fig.
35
[with
V_{0}(q)={1\over 2}kq^{2}
and
V_{1}(q)={1\over 2}k(q-q_{1})^{2}-\varepsilon
].

\Question

Show that when
l=1,\ldots,P-1
, a Trotter product discretisation of

e^{-\beta F(\lambda_{l})}={\text{tr}}\left[e^{-\lambda_{l}\hat{H}_{1}}e^{-(\beta-\lambda_{l})\hat{H}_{0}}\right]

(265)

can be written in the form of Eq.
258
with the Hamiltonian
H_{P}^{(l)}({\mathbf{p}},{\mathbf{q}})
in Eq.
261
.

\Question

Verify the expressions for
-\beta F^{\prime}(\lambda_{l})
and
-\beta F^{\prime\prime}(\lambda_{l})
in Eqs.
262
and
263
.

29
Interpolation Formula

Finally, consider an electronically non-adiabatic reaction in which the electronic coupling matrix element
\Delta
is too large to be treated as a perturbation and yet too small for the reaction to be assumed to proceed solely on the ground adiabatic Born-Oppenheimer potential energy surface, as illustrated in Fig.
37
(c).

Figure 37:
Various regimes for an electronically non-adiabatic reaction. The diabatic electronic potential energies
V_{0}(q)
and
V_{1}(q)
are shown in solid black, and the adiabatic energies
V_{\pm}(q)=\bar{V}(q)\pm{1\over 2}\sqrt{\delta V(q)^{2}+4\Delta^{2}}
in dashed red, both as functions of the diabatic energy gap
\delta V(q)=V_{0}(q)-V_{1}(q)
. (a) The adiabatic limit where
\beta\Delta\gg 1
, the upper electronic adiabat is thermally inaccessible, and the Born-Oppenheimer rate on the lower adiabat is all that is required. (b) The non-adiabatic limit where
\beta\Delta\ll 1
and Fermi’s Golden Rule (second order perturbation theory in
\Delta
) gives the correct rate. (c) The intermediate regime where
\beta\Delta\simeq 1
and neither approximation is justified (on its own).

Neither RPMD rate theory nor Wolynes theory can be used to solve this problem. However, an effective solution can be constructed by combining the two methods, as illustrated in Fig.
38
. This shows the Fermi Golden Rule rate,
k_{\text{GR}}(\Delta)
, the Born-Oppenheimer rate,
k_{\text{BO}}(\Delta)
, and the exact quantum mechanical rate,
k(\Delta)
, of a typical electronically non-adiabatic reaction as a function of the electronic coupling strength. The Golden Rule rate is exact for small
\Delta
but increases as
\Delta^{2}
, resulting in an over-estimation of the exact rate at large
\Delta
. The Born-Oppenheimer rate is exact for large
\Delta
but decreases monotonically to a plateau value at small
\Delta
. The plateau value is the adiabatic rate on the cusped potential
V_{\text{min}}(q)=\min[V_{0}(q),V_{1}(q)]
, and the behaviour of
k_{\text{BO}}(\Delta)
for larger
\Delta
can be understood in terms of the decrease in the ground adiabatic reaction barrier (and hence the Born-Oppenheimer activation energy) with increasing
\Delta
.

Figure 38:
Illustration of the transition from non-adiabatic (Fermi Golden Rule) to adiabatic (Born-Oppenheimer) behaviour of an electron transfer rate constant (red curve), as a function of
\beta\Delta
. Note the logarithmic scales on both axes.

These observations suggest making a Padé-like approximation to
k(\Delta)
,

k(\Delta)\simeq{k_{\text{BO}}(0)k_{\text{GR}}(\Delta)\over k_{\text{BO}}(\Delta)+k_{\text{GR}}(\Delta)},

(266)

where
k_{\text{BO}}(0)
is the adiabatic rate on the cusped potential. As
\Delta\to 0
, the denominator in this expression becomes dominated by
k_{\text{BO}}(\Delta\to 0)
, which then cancels with the
k_{\text{BO}}(0)
in the numerator to leave
k(T)\simeq k_{\text{GR}}(\Delta)
, the correct non-adiabatic rate constant. And at large
\Delta
, the denominator becomes dominated by
k_{\text{GR}}(\Delta)
, which then cancels with the
k_{\text{GR}}(\Delta)
in the numerator to leave
k(T)\simeq k_{\text{BO}}(\Delta)
, the correct adiabatic rate constant. Moreover both limits are approached from below, consistent with the behaviour of the exact rate constant in Fig.
38
:
k(T)
approaches
k_{\text{GR}}(T)
from below as
\Delta\to 0
and
k_{\text{BO}}(T)
from below at large
\Delta
. One can also show that Eq.
266
satisfies detailed balance (i.e., that
k_{f}(\Delta)/k_{b}(\Delta)=K
, where
k_{f}(\Delta)
and
k_{b}(\Delta)
are the forward and backward rate constants and
K
is the equilibrium constant for the reaction), and that it reduces to the well-known Zusmann formula in the appropriate (strong friction and classical nuclear motion) regime
[
134
]
.

Since RPMD rate theory can be used to calculate both
k_{\text{BO}}(\Delta)
and
k_{\text{BO}}(0)
in Eq.
266
, and Wolynes theory to calculate
k_{\text{GR}}(\Delta)
, there is no problem in implementing this interpolation formula with well-established imaginary time path integral techniques. And when one does this, one finds that the error in the interpolation formula is no larger than the error in RPMD rate theory in the Born-Oppenheimer limit or the error in Wolynes theory in the Golden Rule limit, for a wide variety of electron transfer and other electronically non-adiabatic reactions
[
134
]
. The interpolation formula is also physically motivated and appealingly simple. I doubt there will ever be a better or more practical way to use path integral methods to calculate electronically non-adiabatic reaction rates in the intermediate coupling regime.

30
Additional Comments and Further Reading

Since Wolynes theory is a purely imaginary time path integral method, it is just as easy to implement for complex reactions in the Golden Rule limit as RPMD rate theory is in the Born-Oppenheimer limit. Indeed the theory was used to study a fully atomistic model of aqueous ferrous-ferric electron transfer soon after it was first published
[
139
]
, and an atomistic model of biochemical electron transfer just one year later
[
140
]
.

One possible issue with Wolynes theory is that its path integral implementation can only be used directly when the saddle point
\lambda_{\mathrm{sp}}
lies in the range
0\leq\lambda_{\mathrm{sp}}\leq\beta
. This seems to exclude calculations in the Marcus inverted regime for reasons explained in the answer to Exercise
1. However, we have recently shown that the theory can be analytically continued into the inverted regime using path integral information calculated in the normal regime, giving a rate that is just as accurate in the inverted regime as it is in the normal regime
[
138
]
. We have also proposed an alternative “linear golden rule” (LGR) approximation to the rate constant that uses the same ingredients as Wolynes theory but avoids the need for analytic continuation
[
141
]
, and shown that both this and Wolynes theory provide a realistic description of nuclear quantum effects in condensed phase electron transfer reactions
[
142
]
. This was originally established for the Wolynes theory description of aqueous ferrous-ferric electron transfer by Chandler and coworkers over thirty years ago
[
139
]
, and it is just as true today as it was then (despite a recent misguided suggestion to the contrary, which we shall save the authors the embarrassment of citing here).

The interpolation formula discussed in Sec.
29
has so far only been validated for some simple scattering and spin-boson models: it not yet been used in an atomistic simulation. This is a pity, because the formula is both very simple and entirely general, and it is expected to work extremely well
[
134
]
. It should be straightforward to apply the formula in conjunction with RPMD rate theory and Wolynes theory to any condensed phase non-adiabatic reaction with a Hamiltonian of the form in Eq.
238
, and also to allow for non-Condon effects by letting
\Delta\to\Delta(\hat{q})
. If this were combined with machine learning two-state “empirial valence bond” Hamiltonians of the form in Eq.
238
from
ab intio
data, I imagine it could make quite an interesting research project.

Exercise answers

{Answer}

[ref=ex:rate-exercise4]

1.

Inserting Eq.
244
into Eq.
245
gives

\displaystyle c_{ff}(t)={\Delta^{2}\over\hbar^{2}}{\text{tr}}\Bigl[\,

\displaystyle\left<{0}\right|e^{-i\hat{H}t/\hbar}e^{-\beta\hat{H}}\left|{0}\right>\left<{1}\right|e^{+i\hat{H}t/\hbar}\left|{1}\right>

\displaystyle+

\displaystyle\left<{1}\right|e^{-i\hat{H}t/\hbar}e^{-\beta\hat{H}}\left|{1}\right>\left<{0}\right|e^{+i\hat{H}t/\hbar}\left|{0}\right>

\displaystyle-

\displaystyle\left<{1}\right|e^{-i\hat{H}t/\hbar}e^{-\beta\hat{H}}\left|{0}\right>\left<{1}\right|e^{+i\hat{H}t/\hbar}\left|{0}\right>

\displaystyle-

\displaystyle\left<{0}\right|e^{-i\hat{H}t/\hbar}e^{-\beta\hat{H}}\left|{1}\right>\left<{0}\right|e^{+i\hat{H}t/\hbar}\left|{1}\right>\,\Bigr].

Combining this with the fact that
\left<{i}\right|e^{-\alpha\hat{H}}\left|{i}\right>=e^{-\alpha\hat{H}_{i}}+O(\Delta^{2})
and

\left<{i}\right|e^{-\alpha\hat{H}}\left|{j}\right>=-\alpha\Delta+O(\Delta^{2})
for
i\not=j
(both of which can be verified by using Eq.
238
for
\hat{H}
and expanding the exponential operators as Taylor series in
-\alpha\hat{H}
) immediately gives

\displaystyle c_{ff}(t)={\Delta^{2}\over\hbar^{2}}\left[c_{0}(t)+c_{1}(t)+O(\Delta^{2})\right],

where
c_{0}(t)
and
c_{1}(t)
are defined in Eqs.
249
and
251
.

2.

When
\hat{H}_{0}
has discrete eigenstates
\hat{H}_{0}\left|{\nu}\right>=E_{\nu}\left|{\nu}\right>
and
\hat{H}_{1}
has a continuous spectrum, we can evaluate the time integral of
c_{0}(t)
as

\displaystyle\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}c_{0}(t)\,{\mathrm{d}}t

\displaystyle=\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}{\text{tr}}\left[e^{-\beta\hat{H}_{0}}e^{+i\hat{H}_{1}t/\hbar}e^{-i\hat{H}_{0}t/\hbar}\right]\,{\mathrm{d}}t

\displaystyle=\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\sum_{\nu}\left<{\nu}\right|e^{-\beta E_{\nu}}\int{\mathrm{d}}E\,\delta(E-\hat{H}_{1})\,e^{+iEt/\hbar}\,e^{-iE_{\nu}t/\hbar}\left|{\nu}\right>\,{\mathrm{d}}t

\displaystyle=2\pi\hbar\sum_{\nu}\left<{\nu}\right|e^{-\beta E_{\nu}}\int{\mathrm{d}}E\,\delta(E-\hat{H}_{1})\,\delta(E-E_{\nu})\left|{\nu}\right>

\displaystyle=2\pi\hbar\sum_{\nu}e^{-\beta E_{\nu}}\left<{\nu}\right|\delta(E_{\nu}-\hat{H}_{1})\left|{\nu}\right>,

and the time integral of
c_{1}(t)
as

\displaystyle\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}c_{1}(t)\,{\mathrm{d}}t

\displaystyle=\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}{\text{tr}}\left[e^{-\beta\hat{H}_{1}}e^{-i\hat{H}_{1}t/\hbar}e^{+i\hat{H}_{0}t/\hbar}\right]\,{\mathrm{d}}t

\displaystyle=\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\sum_{\nu}\left<{\nu}\right|\int{\mathrm{d}}E\,\delta(E-\hat{H}_{1})\,e^{-\beta E}\,e^{-iEt/\hbar}\,e^{+iE_{\nu}t/\hbar}\left|{\nu}\right>\,{\mathrm{d}}t

\displaystyle=2\pi\hbar\sum_{\nu}\left<{\nu}\right|\int{\mathrm{d}}E\,\delta(E-\hat{H}_{1})\,e^{-\beta E}\,\delta(E-E_{\nu})\left|{\nu}\right>

\displaystyle=2\pi\hbar\sum_{\nu}e^{-\beta E_{\nu}}\left<{\nu}\right|\delta(E_{\nu}-\hat{H}_{1})\left|{\nu}\right>,

both of which give the same result.

It also follows from this argument that we can use a standard formula from quantum scattering theory,

\displaystyle\delta(E-\hat{H}_{1})=-{1\over\pi}{\mathrm{Im}}\,\hat{G}_{1}^{+}(E),

where

\displaystyle\hat{G}_{1}^{+}(E)=\lim_{\eta\to 0_{+}}\left(E+i\eta-\hat{H}_{1}\right)^{-1},

to calculate the Golden Rule rate constant in Eq.
247
as
[
138
]

\displaystyle k(T)=-{2\Delta^{2}\over\hbar Q_{r}(T)}\sum_{\nu}e^{-\beta E_{\nu}}{\mathrm{Im}}\left<{\nu}\right|\hat{G}_{1}^{+}(E_{\nu})\left|{\nu}\right>.

This actually provides quite a practical way to calculate quantum mechanical Golden Rule rate constants for gas phase electronic predissociation problems.

3.

For the classical limit of the one-dimensional spin-boson model in Fig.
35
with
V_{0}(q)={1\over 2}kq^{2}
and
V_{1}(q)={1\over 2}k(q-q_{1})^{2}-\varepsilon
, we have

\displaystyle Q_{r}(T)

\displaystyle={1\over 2\pi\hbar}\int{\mathrm{d}}p\int{\mathrm{d}}q\,e^{-\beta[p^{2}/2m+V_{0}(q)]}

\displaystyle={1\over\beta\hbar}\sqrt{m\over k},

and

\displaystyle c_{0}(t)

\displaystyle={1\over 2\pi\hbar}\int{\mathrm{d}}p\int{\mathrm{d}}q\,e^{-\beta[p^{2}/2m+V_{0}(q)]}e^{-i[V_{0}(q)-V_{1}(q)]t/\hbar}

\displaystyle={1\over\hbar}\sqrt{m\over 2\pi\beta}\int{\mathrm{d}}q\,e^{-\beta V_{0}(q)}e^{-ikq_{1}(q-q^{\ddagger})t/\hbar},

where
q^{\ddagger}=(\Lambda-\varepsilon)/kq_{1}
with
\Lambda={1\over 2}kq_{1}^{2}
. Therefore

\displaystyle\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}c_{0}(t)\,{\mathrm{d}}t

\displaystyle={1\over\hbar}\sqrt{m\over 2\pi\beta}\int{\mathrm{d}}q\,e^{-\beta V_{0}(q)}{2\pi\hbar\over kq_{1}}\delta(q-q^{\ddagger})

\displaystyle=\sqrt{m\over k}\sqrt{2\pi\over\beta kq_{1}^{2}}\,e^{-\beta V_{0}(q^{\ddagger})}

\displaystyle=\sqrt{m\over k}\sqrt{\pi\over\beta\Lambda}e^{-\beta(\Lambda-\varepsilon)^{2}/4\Lambda},

which gives the standard Marcus theory result

\displaystyle k(T)

\displaystyle={\Delta^{2}\over\hbar^{2}Q_{r}(T)}\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}c_{0}(t)\,{\mathrm{d}}t

\displaystyle={\Delta^{2}\over\hbar}\sqrt{\pi\beta\over\Lambda}e^{-\beta(\Lambda-\varepsilon)^{2}/4\Lambda}.

{Answer}

[ref=ex:rate-exercise5]

1.

Here we are again considering the classical limit of a one-dimensional spin-boson model with
V_{0}(q)={1\over 2}kq^{2}
and
V_{1}(q)={1\over 2}k(q-q_{1})^{2}-\varepsilon
, so we can steal some of the intermediate results from Exercise
3. In particular, the classical limit of
Q_{r}(T)
is

\displaystyle Q_{r}(T)={1\over\beta\hbar}\sqrt{m\over k}

and the classical limit of
e^{-\beta F(\lambda)}=c_{0}(i\lambda\hbar)
is

\displaystyle e^{-\beta F(\lambda)}

\displaystyle={1\over\hbar}\sqrt{m\over 2\pi\beta}\int{\mathrm{d}}q\,e^{-\beta V_{0}(q)}e^{+\lambda[V_{0}(q)-V_{1}(q)]}

\displaystyle={1\over\hbar}\sqrt{m\over 2\pi\beta}\int{\mathrm{d}}q\,e^{-\beta kq^{2}/2+\lambda[kq_{1}(q-q^{\ddagger})]}

\displaystyle={1\over\beta\hbar}\sqrt{m\over k}e^{+\lambda^{2}kq_{1}^{2}/2\beta-\lambda kq_{1}q^{\ddagger}}

\displaystyle=Q_{r}(T)\,e^{+\Lambda\lambda^{2}/\beta-[\Lambda-\varepsilon]\lambda},

where
\Lambda={1\over 2}kq_{1}^{2}
and
\Lambda-\varepsilon=kq_{1}q^{\ddagger}
. Hence

\displaystyle F(\lambda)=F(0)-{\Lambda\over\beta^{2}}\lambda^{2}+{[\Lambda-\varepsilon]\over\beta}\lambda,

where
e^{-\beta F(0)}=Q_{r}(T)
. This is all we need to evaluate the Wolynes theory rate constant in Eq.
255
.

The saddle point condition
F^{\prime}(\lambda_{\mathrm{sp}})=0
gives

\displaystyle\lambda_{\mathrm{sp}}={\beta\over 2}\left(1-{\varepsilon\over\Lambda}\right),

which decreases from
\lambda_{\mathrm{sp}}=\beta/2
for symmetric electron transfer (
\epsilon=0
) to
\lambda_{\mathrm{sp}}=0
for activationless electron transfer (
\epsilon=\Lambda
), and becomes negative in the Marcus inverted regime (
\epsilon>\Lambda
). The implications of this are discussed in Sec.
30
.

The remainder of the calculation is simply to note that
F(\lambda_{\mathrm{sp}})={[\Lambda-\varepsilon]/4\Lambda}
and
F^{\prime\prime}(\lambda_{\mathrm{sp}})=-{2\Lambda/\beta}
give

\displaystyle k(T)

\displaystyle={\Delta^{2}\over\hbar Q_{r}(T)}\sqrt{2\pi\over-\beta F^{\prime\prime}(\lambda_{\mathrm{sp}})}e^{-\beta F(\lambda_{\mathrm{sp}})}

\displaystyle={\Delta^{2}\over\hbar}\sqrt{\pi\beta\over\Lambda}e^{-\beta(\Lambda-\varepsilon)^{2}/4\Lambda},

and thus that Wolynes theory does indeed give the correct (Marcus theory) result in the classical limit for the spin-boson model.

2.

The Trotter product discretisation is based on the approximation

\displaystyle e^{-\beta_{P}\hat{H}}\simeq e^{-\beta_{P}\hat{V}/2}e^{-\beta_{P}\hat{T}}e^{-\beta_{P}\hat{V}/2},

which has an error of
O(\beta_{P}^{3})
and gives

\displaystyle\left<{q}\right|e^{-\beta_{P}\hat{H}}\left|{q^{\prime}}\right>

\displaystyle=e^{-\beta_{P}V(q)/2}\left<{q}\right|e^{-\beta_{P}\hat{T}}\left|{q^{\prime}}\right>e^{-\beta_{P}V(q^{\prime})/2}

\displaystyle=\int{\mathrm{d}}p\,\left<q|p\right>e^{-\beta_{P}p^{2}/2m}\left<p|q^{\prime}\right>e^{-\beta_{P}[V(q)+V(q^{\prime})]/2}

\displaystyle={1\over 2\pi\hbar}\int{\mathrm{d}}p\,e^{-\beta_{P}p^{2}/2m+ip(q-q^{\prime})/\hbar}e^{-\beta_{P}[V(q)+V(q^{\prime})]/2}

\displaystyle={1\over 2\pi\hbar}\sqrt{2\pi m\over\beta_{P}}\,e^{-\beta_{P}[m\omega_{P}^{2}(q-q^{\prime})^{2}/2+V(q)/2+V(q^{\prime})/2]}

\displaystyle={1\over 2\pi\hbar}\int{\mathrm{d}}p\,e^{-\beta_{P}[p^{2}/2m+m\omega_{P}^{2}(q-q^{\prime})^{2}/2+V(q)/2+V(q^{\prime})/2]},

with
\omega_{P}=1/(\beta_{P}\hbar)
.

In order to derive Eq. (1.64), we simply use this result
P
times:

\displaystyle e^{-\beta_{P}F(\lambda_{l})}

\displaystyle={\text{tr}}\left[e^{-\lambda_{l}\hat{H}_{1}}e^{-(\beta-\lambda_{l})\hat{H}_{0}}\right]

\displaystyle=\int{\mathrm{d}}{\mathbf{q}}\left<{q_{1}}\right|e^{-\beta_{P}\hat{H}_{1}}\left|{q_{2}}\right>\cdots\left<{q_{l}}\right|e^{-\beta_{P}\hat{H}_{1}}\left|{q_{l+1}}\right>

\displaystyle\phantom{xxx}\times\left<{q_{l+1}}\right|e^{-\beta_{P}\hat{H}_{0}}\left|{q_{l+2}}\right>\cdots\left<{q_{P}}\right|e^{-\beta_{P}\hat{H}_{0}}\left|{q_{1}}\right>

\displaystyle={1\over(2\pi\hbar)^{P}}\int{\mathrm{d}}{\mathbf{p}}\int{\mathrm{d}}{\mathbf{q}}\,e^{-\beta_{P}H_{P}^{(l)}({\mathbf{p}},{\mathbf{q}})},

with
H_{P}^{(l)}({\mathbf{p}},{\mathbf{q}})
defined as in Eq.
261
.

3.

From the definition of
F(\lambda)
in Eq.
256
,

\displaystyle-\beta F^{\prime}(\lambda)e^{-\beta F(\lambda)}

\displaystyle={{\mathrm{d}}\over{\mathrm{d}}\lambda}{\text{tr}}\left[e^{-(\beta-\lambda)\hat{H}_{0}}e^{-\lambda\hat{H}_{1}}\right]

\displaystyle={\text{tr}}\left[e^{-(\beta-\lambda)\hat{H}_{0}}[\hat{H}_{0}-\hat{H}_{1}]\,e^{-\lambda\hat{H}_{1}}\right]

\displaystyle={\text{tr}}\left[e^{-(\beta-\lambda)\hat{H}_{0}}[V_{0}(\hat{q})-V_{1}(\hat{q})]\,e^{-\lambda\hat{H}_{1}}\right]

\displaystyle={\text{tr}}\left[e^{-(\beta-\lambda)\hat{H}_{0}}\delta V(\hat{q})\,e^{-\lambda\hat{H}_{1}}\right]

\displaystyle={\text{tr}}\left[\delta V(\hat{q})\,e^{-\lambda\hat{H}_{1}}\,e^{-(\beta-\lambda)\hat{H}_{0}}\right],

and therefore

\displaystyle-\beta F^{\prime}(\lambda)

\displaystyle={\displaystyle{{\text{tr}}\left[\delta V(\hat{q})\,e^{-\lambda\hat{H}_{1}}\,e^{-(\beta-\lambda)\hat{H}_{0}}\right]}\over\displaystyle{{\text{tr}}\left[e^{-\lambda\hat{H}_{1}}\,e^{-(\beta-\lambda)\hat{H}_{0}}\right]}}.

Differentiating again and using the same argument gives

\displaystyle-\beta F^{\prime\prime}(\lambda)

\displaystyle={\displaystyle{{\text{tr}}\left[\delta V(\hat{q})\,e^{-\lambda\hat{H}_{1}}\,\delta V(\hat{q})\,e^{-(\beta-\lambda)\hat{H}_{0}}\right]}\over\displaystyle{{\text{tr}}\left[e^{-\lambda\hat{H}_{1}}\,e^{-(\beta-\lambda)\hat{H}_{0}}\right]}}-{\displaystyle{{\text{tr}}\left[\delta V(\hat{q})\,e^{-\lambda\hat{H}_{1}}\,e^{-(\beta-\lambda)\hat{H}_{0}}\right]^{2}}\over\displaystyle{{\text{tr}}\left[e^{-\lambda\hat{H}_{1}}\,e^{-(\beta-\lambda)\hat{H}_{0}}\right]^{2}}}.

Equations
262
to
264
in the text simply implement these expressions for
\lambda_{l}=l\beta_{P}
with
l=0,\ldots,P
, where they can be written in terms of canonical averages of
\delta V(q_{1})
and
\delta V(q_{1})\delta V(q_{l+1})
in the system with the ring polymer Hamiltonian
H_{P}^{(l)}({\mathbf{p}},{\mathbf{q}})
.
